DEPARTMENT OF STATE

a knowledge of Japanese culture. Most
of these "second-generation" Japanese
are young people of high-school or
university age and the bulk of them
attend some sort of Japanese school.
Although some find employment others
merely waste their time and money.
Several clubs and societies have been
organized among these young people, the
Pacific Young People's Society having the
Iargest membership. There are also
several schools set up especially for them.
Among these the Institute for Education
of Over-sea Japanese has recently been
Founded by the Japanese Government. The
Y.M.C.A. and the Y.W.C.A. also take an
active interest in and provide facilities
for these young people.
Being by training and education foreign
to all things Japanese, they are
necessarily faced with the problems of
any foreigner going to Japan. However,
being of the Japanese race they are more
often than not expected to think, speak,
and act as Japanese, and hence do not
receive the sympathy or social immunity
usually granted foreigners of another
race. Their lack of sufficient resources
forces them to plunge immediately into
the Japanese mode of life and denies
them

=== Page 4 === (confidence 0.98)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
- 3 -
them what few diversions and examples of
western life there are in Japan.  The
result is that many become disillusioned
and discouraged and return to their homes
unfavorably Impressed with Japan and
Japanese culture. The above-mentioned
societies and schools are attempting to
cushion the first shock and orient these
"second-generation" Japanese to their new
life, and to present to them an agreeable
and favorable view of Japanese culture.
The Japanese Government in line with
its desire to spread Japanese culture, and
political and economic influence has become
interested in this group. Obviously,
if many of these "second-generation"
Japanese go to Japan and do return after
a relatively short time unfavorably impres-
sed with the Japanese culture, they will
tend to discredit much of the propaganda
of the Japanese Government especially among
other "second-generation" Japanese in the
United States and Hawaii.
In 1930 there were some 159,542
"second-generation" Japanese in Hawaii
and the United States. It is estimated
that probably about 40,000 second-genera-
tion Japanese were living in Japan in
1930 and that about 1,000 of these were
of the group discussed in this report.

THE SECOND-GENERATION JAPANESE PROBLEM
INTRODUCTION
"Second-generation Japanese" is the term used to
denote persons of Japanese race born in American ter-
ritory. It inoludes those born in the Hawaiian Is-
lands as well as those born in the continental United
States. It includes (a) those who have formally for-
sworn American citizenship or renounced it in effect;
(b) those who claim dual citizenship; and (c) those
who have forsworn Japanese citizenship.
As the term is so inclusive it is not always con-
sidered as being complimentary either by those who con-
sider themselves out-and-out American citizens or by
those who have claimed only Japanese nationality.
It is possible that other Americans may also some-
times use it in a derogatory way. In this report the
term will be used in its broadest sense as applying
to all American-born children of Japanese parents,
whether living in the United States, Hewaii, or Japan,
and whether they have American, Japanese, or dual
nationality. Needless to say no derogatory meaning is
implied.
The continental United States will be referred to
simply as the United States. For purposes of convenience,
the Hawaiian Islands, although an integral part of the
territory of the United States, will be referred to as
Hawaii, and separately from the continental United
States

=== Page 10 === (confidence 0.97)
一 8 -
States, it being understood that persons born in the
Hawaiian Islands are native-born American citizens.
The reason for separate treatment of the Hawaiian Is-
lands is that the Japanese population, both alien and
oitizen, is quite distinct from the communities on
the Pacific coast of the United States.
Previous reports upon the presence in Japan of
American citizens of Japanese race have been made by
Erle R. Dickover, once while Consul at Kobe, in his
811.111 Quota 94/103
despatoh No. 1096 of September 30, 1931, and again
while Secretary of Embassy at Tokyo in despatch No.
811.111 Q0294127
438 of June 20, 1933. Vice Consul R. M. McClintock,
when assigned to Kobe, also submitted a report upon
the subjoct on August 23, 1934. 13 0 Reinion//88
This report is chiefly concerned with those de-
signated as "Group C" second-generation Japanese, as
described in the following section on page 5.
* * *

=== Page 11 === (confidence 0.99)
-3 -
DESCRIPTION OF SECOND-GENERATION JAPANESE
Second-goneration Japanese now in Japan may be
divided into three classes, as follows: Group A, those
who have forsworn American citizenship formally or
renounced it in effect; Group B, those who claim either
American citizenship alone or dual nationality, but
who have been brought to Japan at an early age by their
parents; and Group C, those who claim either American
oitizenship or dual nationality, have completed pri-
mary education in United States or Hawaii, and have
come to Japan with a definite American background.
Group A, those who have forsworn American ci-
tizenship by formal act of themselves or parents, or
do not consider themselves American citizens, will
not be discussed in this report. There are many of
them, most of whose parents are high-class business-
men or Japanese Government officials who were living
temporarily in the United States when the children
were born. Although they may have been educated in
the United States they have always considered them-
selves Japanese subjects and have conducted them-
selves accordingly.
Those in Group B, who were brought at an early
age to Japan by their parents, have been discussed at
some length by Mr. Dickover and Mr. McClintock in the
reports referred to in the Introduotion to this report.
These Americans are thoroughly Japanese: they
neither speak nor read English and have no acquaintance
with

=== Page 12 === (confidence 0.97)
一 4 -
with American culture. They live in Japanese homes;
wear Japanese olothing; eat Japanese food; work in
Japanese fields or factories; and are in everything
but place of birth and citizenship pure Japanese.
They generally claim Japanese as well as American
citizenship even though they may obtain American
passports. The purpose of the passports and claim
to American citizenship is apparently to ensure their
right to return to the United States to avoid mili-
tary service, and does not indicate anything more
than that. Some of them, only a small percentage,
are even registered as American citizens at the
various American consulates in Japan for the same
reason.
As suggested by the officers who submitted the
aforementioned reports, these people make an interest-
ing problem for the labor interests on the Pacific
coast of the United States. As American citizens they
cannot be kept out of the United States if and when
7
they choose to return. As typical Japanese coolies
they are quite as capable of competing with white
laborers as their parents were. Furthermore, their
children, though born in Japan, may also enter the
United States as citizens. It is believed that only
a few of them have children at this date as they are
mostly quite young. As the years pass, however, the
number of ohildren may grow to be considerable.
On the other hand, unless such citizens return to
the United States or send their children to the United
States

=== Page 13 === (confidence 0.99)
- 5 -
States in accordance with the provisions of the Aot
of May 24, 1934, many of them will merge permanently
into the population of Japan. It is fair to assume
that a large proportion may never return to the United
States. Certainly they will remain in this country
while conditions are so unfavorable in the United
States and comparatively better in Japan. From the
point of view of the Japanese Government they are
Japanese subjects until they make formal action re-
nouncing Japanese citizenship. This is rarely done
by those who have come to Japan while very young.
Group C is composed of those who have spent the
formative years of their lives in the United States
or Hawaii. These are the persons with whom this re-
port is especially concerned.
The characteristics of this group are generally
as follows:

more of these are scattered in other
parts of Japan.

REASONS FOR COMING TO JAPAN
There are several reasons for the comparatively
large number of Group c (see page 5) second-generation
Japanese coming to Japan during the last four or five
years. The economic depression in the United States
is the most important cause. In times of unemploy-
ment and when there is an emphasis upon giving the
limited jobs to "loo% Americans," it is more difficult
than ever for "foreigners" whether they are Japanese-
Americans, Philippine-Americans, Mexican-Americans or
any other race physically distinguishable. If times
had been better and jobs had been available to the young
Japanese-Americans coming out of schools and colleges
there would have been no such movement to Japan.
While the movement to Japan no doubt originated
in the unemployment problems at home, the low value of
the yen in terms of dollars has made it possible for
the parents of the young people to send them over at
comparatively little expenditure. Most of them come
to Japan on Japanese vessels as either second or third
class passengers. It is possible, eccording to figures
worked out by the Tokyo Y. M. C. A., for a young man to
live in Tokyo for Yen 75.00 a month. This equals about
$21.00 a month at the present rate of exchange. It
allows for no extravagance whatever: Yen 25.00 for a room
at the Y. M. C. A.; Yen 30.00 for food (strictly Japanese
food with no extras); and Yen 20.00 a month for clothes,
transportation

=== Page 16 === (confidence 0.98)
-7 -
transportation, amusement, eto. Tuition at the various
schools is very low. According to figures given by
Waseda International Institute a student can obtain
room and board for Yen 30.00 a month, tuition for
Yen 15.00; and all other expenses will come to only
Yon 20.00 a.month - a total of Yen 65.00 a month (about
18.00): The section on "Schools" in this report con-
tains further data concerning cost of living for stu-
dents. A girl, according to the Tokyo Y. W. C. A.,
needs more than Yen lo0.00 a month to live by herself.
This is equivalent to about $30.00 at the present rate
of exchange. If the young people are alone it is con-
s idered advisable that they have only just enough to
get along, as it keeps them out of mischief. It can
be seen that a Japanese parent in the United States or
Hawaii can much better support an unemployed child and
provide an education as well by sending him to Japan
for three or four years than by send ing him to college
in the United States. It is an inexpensive way of
keeping him interested in life while there is nothing
to do at home.
The desire of Japanese parents to have their
children go to Japan to learn at first hand samething
of Japanese culture is also involved. The desire is
considered a practical one, since positions with ex-
porting and shipping firms on the Pacific coast are
supposed to be more easily obtained by Japanese-Amer-
icans who can speak, read, and write Japanese.
According to replies to questionnaires rece ived
from

=== Page 17 === (confidence 0.97)
-8 -
from approximately one hundred of the second-generation
Japanese, two thirds of them came to Japan for the pur-
pose of study. Nearly all the rest came because their
parents returned to Japan, while only a few came to
Japan for the purpose of finding work. A larger pro-
portion of girls than boys came back with their parents.
Very few of the boys, in fact, have their parents in
Japan. It is said that some of the parents who brought
their American-born daughters to Japan hoped to find
Japanese husbands for them and to remain in Japan.
American girls, whether of Japanese or occidental race,
are too independent to suit Japanese husbands, however,
and very few of the girls have found husbands. Many of
them find employment as stenographers and typists, as
stated elsewhere in this report.
* * *

=== Page 18 === (confidence 0.99)
- 9 -
ACTIVITIES
Although the Tokyo Y. W. C. A. received only lo0
replies to a questionnaire sent to 450 young Americans
of Japanese parentage living in the Tokyo-Yokohama
area, it is believed that the conclusions drawn from
the answers received may be fairly generally applied
to the entire group. According to the answers, 70
percent of them are studying, most of the rest are
working, and the remainder are at loose ends. In the
section of this report devoted to "Schools" their stu-
dent activities are described in detail. As stated in
"Reasons for Coming to Japan," the low fees for tuition
and the low exohange value of the yen has made it pos-
sible for Japanese parents in the United States to send
their children to Japan to study for less expenditure
than would be required at home, and at the same time
give them the opportunity of acquiring a knowledge of
the Japanese language and culture. Most of them who
manage to stay in Japan over a year take their studies
very seriously and are acquiring a great deal of know-
ledge which they hope will be of use to them in the
United States.
Those who are working have found employment in all
sorts of occupations. Many of the girls have found it
comparatively easy to obtain positions as stenographers
in the offices of American companies in Tokyo and Yoko-
hama and in the offices of large Japanese companies which
have

=== Page 19 === (confidence 0.99)
-10 -
have business with the United States. The young men
have had a harder time finding employment, but a number
have also succeeded with American firms or Japanese
firms with American export business. One young man is
working as an interpreter in the Japanese Customhouse
at Yokohama, teaching new customs examiners how to deal
with American tourists.
However, unless the young men have family connec-
tions with influence in Japan or have outstending qual-
ities themselves, they have great difriculty in obtain-
ing employment. The competition for office jobs is very
keen. There is the further fact that wages, by Amer-
1oan standards, are very low. One hundred yen (say
thirty dollars) a month is a very good wage for office
employees in Japan. In fact the wage for the average
family in Japan is just under Yen 100.00 a month.
An interesting phase of the second generation in
Japan is the success of a number of them, mostly young
women, who have been employed as "jazz singers" and
"orooners." American dance music, "orooning," and "jazz"
orohestra ere in great demand in Tokyo and Yokohama.
Despite the efforts of Japanese performers and orchestras
to provide such entertainment, the latest innovations as
rendered by American performers are always in demand, and
those who are of Japanese race are preferred by the Japa-
nese themselves.
One second-generation girl took up the ancient Japa-
nese drama, called "kabuki," with such success that she is
to be adopted into a family of "kabuki" actors.
In

=== Page 20 === (confidence 0.98)
- 11-
In general it may be said that Japan does not
offer a large field of employment for the second.
generation.
Those who have no definite purpose in mind pre-
sent a problem for the organizations interested in the
welfare of the young people, especially the young men,
and there seems to be a large number of them, whose
parents have sent them over with an allowance suffi-
oient for them to live without working and without re-
quiring them to attend any of the schools open to them.
It is feared that they will spend their funds and their
time in places of ill repute and bring unpleasant no-
toriety upon the rest of the second generation. The
Y. M. C. A. and the Y. W. C. A. have tried to reach them
and have succeeded in many cases. But there must be a
great many who are wasting their time and their parents'
money. They generally return to the United States with-
in a few months.
* * *

=== Page 21 === (confidence 0.99)
-12 -
SOCTETIES
Since the number of young people described above
as Group C began to be noticeable two or three years
ago, various people, Japanese,second-generation, and
other Americans have tried to organize clubs among
them. Several olubs were organized and petered out.
At present the two principal clubs are the Pacific
Young People's Society and the Japan Cultural Forum.
The Pacifio Young People's Society has the larg-
est membership --- about sixty young people --- fairly
evenly divided between boys and girls. Its success
has been due to the fact that the purposes of the olub
a1
are social and recreation, and the members have avoid-
ed trying to give it a cultural mission. As most of
the young people spend the larger part of their time
studying the Japanese language and culture, and as they
are living in Japanese surroundings, their greatest
outside need is relaxation and relief from their new
environment. They want to meet toget her and throw off
the burdens of study and the surveillance of Japanese
relatives and friends: to "be themselves" in exactly
the same way as other foreigners in Japan do in their
"foreign colony" activit ies. The Society therefore
organizes frequent "socials," pionios, and dances.
To such activities it draws not only its members but
the friends of members, i.e., other second-generation
young people who for one reason or another have not
joined

=== Page 22 === (confidence 0.99)
-13 -
joined the Society.
The Society has advisors among the Americans in
Tokyo, inoluding members of the staffs of the Tokyo
Y. M. C. A. and the Tokyo Y. W. C. A. The advisors
may be responsible for a slight cultural tendenoy of
late, whioh resulted in the production of one of the
classical Japanese plays, with traditional Japanese
costume and scenery but in the English language. The
purpose of the play was to raise funds for the Society
for "cooperative cultural projects, for meetings with
local student groups, for special inspection trips to
historio spots, etc." The permanent American foreign
colonies of Tokyo and Yokohama supported the play by
buying patrons' tickets and the rest of the audience
was made up chiefly of other second-generation young
people.
The Japan Cultural Forum is the other society,
with only eighteen members of both sexes, and its aims
are more serious. Its constitution states that its
purposes "shall be to promote good-will and better
understanding between the United States and Japan through
a deeper appreciation of the cultures of each nation."
It has an American advisor, a missionary in Tokyo, whose
home is used for the meetings twice a month. Its memb-
ers are older than those of the other club. The charac-
ter of the members of the Japan Cultural Forum can be
seen from the description of them in the list enclosed.
The Sooiety has not attempted any publio affair such as
the

=== Page 23 === (confidence 0.98)
-14 -
the play given by the other club, but the Japanese
Government has called upon it for interpreters or
guides upon special occasions when prominent Ameri-
cans have been visiting Japan. The members feel com-
plimented by the attention of the Japanese Government
but do not intend to let the organization lose its
purely American character.
The most serious handicap of both societies is
the transient residence in Japan of their members, which
results in a constant turnover. This difficulty is un-
avoidable, however, and the organizations carry on in
spite of it.
* * *

=== Page 24 === (confidence 0.99)
-15 -
PROBLEMS OF THE SECOND-GENERATION JAPANESE
The second-generation Japanese classified under
Group C, whose baokground is in the United States and
who come after finishing high school or college in the
United States for a few years' study or work in Japan,
have to face much more serious problems than the other
Americans and foreigners who come for either temporary
or permanent residence. The "white" Americans and
foreigners have contacts in the permanent foreign colony
of Tokyo or Yokohama and are able to carry on a life
more or less like that in America or Europe: they live
in foreign style houses, employ servants to prepare
American or European food, and carry on a cosmopolitan
sort of life to which it is comparatively easy to ac-
custom oneself. Finally, the Japanese do not confuse
them with their own people and are consequently ac-
customed to expect a different conduct.
The second-generation young people,on the other
hand, with practically the same background as the other
Americans, are faced from the start with the most dif-
ficult problems. In the first place, their physical ap-
pearance stamps them as Japanese and they are expect-
ed to speak, eat, dress, think and act like Japanese;
and they receive very little sympathy when they soon
show that they must be treated as any other foreigners.
Their knowledge of the Japanese language upon arrival is
generally so slight as to be quite useless. Their funds
are

=== Page 25 === (confidence 0.99)
-16-
are so limited that they cannot afford to live in the
expensive hotels and houses where the other Americans ---
business men, missionaries, tourists --- find refuge.
If they are to visit relatives, the latter are Japanese
of course. If they are to find their own quarters they
must still confine themselves to purely Japanese sur-
roundings, the only kind they can afford.
Regarding the language difficulty, it is just as
hard for a second-generation young man or young woman
to learn Japanese as it is for any other American. It
would be a strain for any of us to live in Japan com-
pletely cut off from any English-speaking persons.
There is also the difficulty of becoming accustom-
ed to Japanese food. The average foreigner living in
Japan eats very little Japanese food, no matter how
many years he may live here. The diet is radically dif-
ferent from American diet and a sudden change to it
usually brings severe digestive trouble. The second-
generation must and do make such a change, and it can
be due only to their youth and uniformly good physique
that they accomplish the change without permanent damage
to their digestive systems. They prefer American food,
however, and look forward to the time when they can re-
turn to the United States and enjoy the food they were
brought up to like.
Japanese homes are as unlike American homes as one
can imagine. Americans who visit Japan, as well as those
who live here many years, occupy American style dwellings
and

=== Page 26 === (confidence 0.99)
-17-
and hotels. Visits to typical Japanese homes, res-
taurants, temples, theatres, etc., are always interest-
ing, but it is difficult to accustom oneself to sit-
ting on the floor habitually. The second-generation
have to begin at once to sit on the floor most of the
day and sleep on the floor at night. The most dif-
fioult feature of living in Japanese dwellings is not
getting used to squatting or sitting on one's feet,
however. The difficult part is to accustom oneself to
live all winter, with the temperature about that of
winter in Washington, D. C., in an unheated house which
is built in so fragile a way that cold air continually
enters from all sides. In addition to that, there is a
general lack of plumbing and of modern sanitary appli-
ances. It does not take a vivid imagination to picture
the depressing effect of spending several cold months,
with a fair amount of wet weather, in a fragile house,
unheated except for a few embers of charcoal; living
on the floor; and eating what seems to be most unap-
petizing food. The second generation do not like it
any more than any other Americans would like it.
As those who have lived in Japan know, Japanese
life is filled with symbolism and an elaborate courtesy
which takes years to learn. The Japanese never expect
foreigners to become accustomed to these fine points of
their culture and foreigners are excused when they make
faux pas which would be resented if one of their own
people committed them. The second-generation, with no
more

=== Page 27 === (confidence 0.99)
-18 -
more knowledge of these things than any other American
and quite as deserving of consideration for their mis-
takes, are not forgiven so readily. Instead they inour
constant criticism. The result is a tension which is
escaped only by returning to the United States.
Finally, the second-generation miss the amusements
which young people at home enjoy, partioularly the social
affairs which high school and college students at home
organize through their clubs and societies, in which
both sexes take part. In Japan the sexes are kept apart
and dancing is not encouraged. In fact, in most parts
of Tokyo it is prohibited by police regulations. It
is only when some American member of the foreign colony
in Tokyo or Yokohama invites them to his home that they
can engage in amusements such as they used to enjoy in
the United States.
Efforts are made to overcome many of these handi-
caps. Some of the young people join together in find-
ing living quarters. A few apartment houses have re-
cently been built where a few pieces of foreign fur-
niture are installed and a certain amount of heat is
furnished in the winter. Some of them may be able to
eat occasionally at the only American style restaurant
in Tokyo which is not so expens ive as the de luxe hotels.
Generally, however, the second-generation "go Japanese"
perforce. It is said that most of them give up very
quickly and return to the United States within a few
months. Others stay longer than a year, however, and
by

=== Page 28 === (confidence 0.97)
- 19 -
by that time are able to carry on until they have
finished their studies.
The question naturally arises as to what effeot
upon the Japanese-Americans in Hawaii and the United
States the return of so many disappointed young people
will have. If, as it is alleged, the majority of them
return within a year, homesick and disillusioned, their
discouraging reports of Japan must soon counteract to
an appreciable extent the propaganda which the Japa-
nese Government is so assiduously spreading by means
of its cultural societies --- at least among the second-
generation Japanese.
* * *

=== Page 29 === (confidence 0.99)
- 20 -
SCHOOLS
Sohool of Japanese Language and Culture
(Nichigo Bunka Gakko)
combined with Sohool for Foreign-born Japanese
This school, which is conducted by the Tokyo
Y. M. C. A., is for high school graduates, and offers
a two year course in written and spoken Japanese as
well as oultural courses. The school requires a mini-
mum of fourteen hours' instruction a week in classes
of not more than five persons each. There is limited
dormitory space. A "personal guidance" service assists
students in finding other living quarters and obtain-
ing employment on graduation. Tuition is Yen 45.00
for a term of twelve weeks. A catalogue of the school
is enolosed.
Waseda International Institute
The Waseda International Institute, which is a
part of Waseda University, prepares "American-born
Japanese and Americans of Japanese lineage" for en-
trance into the University, with especial emphasis
upon the Japanese Language and culture. It requires
five hours of instruction daily and the course is
divided into three terms a year. Students are assist-
ed in finding living quarters and they have access to
the athletio department of the Waseda Christian Center.
Tuition is Yen 60.00 for the autumn and spring terms and
Yen 45.00

=== Page 30 === (confidence 0.99)
- 21 -
Yon 45.00 for the winter term. A oatalogue of the
Institute is enclosed.
Institute for Education of Over-sea Japanese
(Kaigai Kyoiku Kyokai)
This was organized by the Japanese Government
officials, with Viscount Ishii as Chairman of the
Board. The Board includes Mr. Tadashi Sato (parlia-
mentary counsellor of the Over-sea Ministry, who is
exeoutive director of the Institute), the Prime Minis-
ter, the Secretary of Foreign Affairs, and the Minis-
ter of Education. The school was opened on April 20,
1935,and at present has only six students. The fee
for board and room is Yen 30.00 a month and tuition
is Yen 10.00 a month.
The following is an excerpt from the school cata-
logue, a translation of which is enclosed with this
report:
"The institute plans to provide courses adapted
to the needs of each student with the main emphasis on
Japanese language, history, and culture. The activi-
ties of the Institute include, besides the research in
Japanese affairs, a clinic, a department for educational
guidance, a club for Japanese from abroad, and the publi-
cation of magazines and other printed matter.
"If enough students apply, high schools for boys
and girls will be sterted. In case the applications are
insufficient the institute will provide opportunities
and

=== Page 31 === (confidence 0.99)
- 22 -
and lodging in other schools. The institute expects
to complete plans for the establishment of special
schools of commerce, industry, agriculture, and marine
by next year."
The dormitories are located in Kanagawe prefeoture,
on the Tama river, which divides the prefectures of
Tokyo and Kanagawa, several miles from the center of
Tokyo and from any of the universities. They are pleas-
ing in appearance, of frame and stucco construction, and
built upon e high hill overlooking the river valley.
At present the six students are being instructed
daily in Japanese. The students have little to do, by
way of recreation, and do not appear to be particularly
interested in the institution. They are very young.
Two are from the United States, two from Canada, one is
from Mexico, and one from the Philippine Islands.
The projeot is still in its fomative stage and it
is too early yet to s ay what its future will be. It
would seem necessary to have much additional thought and
planning given to it. At present its only adventage to
a second-generation Japanese would be the low cost of
room and board. While this is a large factor in the lives
of most of the young people now studying in Tokyo, the
quality of the instruction and the nearness to the Japa-
nese colleges in the case of those ready for attendance
at the regular educat ional inst itut ion are also of im-
portance.
The interest of Japanese Government officials or
agencies

=== Page 32 === (confidence 0.99)
-23 -
ageneies in such an institution is partly philanthropio
but probably largely for purposes of propaganda.
Meiji University
Although a number of second-generation boys attend
Meiji, there has been no special arrangement of courses
for suoh students. One of the professors, who has liv-
ed in the United States, takes an interest in them to
the extent of acting as their advisor.
Nichibei Home
This is located near the Nakano railway station
in Tokyo. It is primarily a dormitory for second-
generation students, most of whom are from Hawaii.
The director is Mr. Kozen Tsunemitsu, a Buddhist priest,
who has lived several years in the United States. He
colleoted funds from Japanese living around Los Angeles
for the purpose of building suitable living quarters
for their American-born children to use while they are
studying in Japan. The building is new, with accomo-
dations for about thirty young men. The director went
to California, and Hawaii during the summer of l935 to
collect more money and obtain more students, with the
intent ion of enlarging the dormitory.
The idea of Mr. Tsunemitsu has been considered
successful. With the close relationship and personal
contact between him and the parents of the boys in the
dormitory the supervision necessary has been maintained.
The

=== Page 33 === (confidence 0.99)
- 24 -
The study of the Japanese language is the only in-
struotion given in the building. The purpose of this
is to prepare those without sufficient knowledge of
the language to attend the Japanese colleges and uni-
versities.
The institution has the approval of the Inter-
national Cultural Relations Association (Kokusai Bunka
Shinkokai) and may receive some financial assistance
toward obtaining teachers for the preliminary in-
struotion of the new students in the Japanese language.
This project seems very sensible and may very
possibly develop considerably along the same lines it
is now following. It serves as a needed step in ac-
customing the young people to the ways of Japan. Not
only do they get a more gradual introduction to Japa-
nese food, customs, manners, and language than they
could acquire on their own, or by living with friends
or relatives in purely Japanese surroundings, but as
a group of them are living together their problems are
shared and they are spared a great deal of homesickness.
There is an additional point of importance: such an in-
stitution makes it possible for Japanese Governmental
agencies, should they desire to do so, to exert an in-
fluence over the young people. Such an influence would
no doubt be partly in the interests of the young people,
but there would naturally be a certain amount of Govern-
ment propaganda. It is this opening which has perhaps
interested the International Cultural Relations Associa-
tion

=== Page 34 === (confidence 0.99)
- 25 -
tion, mentioned above.
Toyo Nippongo Ka iwagakko
A private sohool, apparently just established.
Its purpose is to "teach the Japanese language to all
foreigners and foreign-born Japanese, and to let them
master it in a very short time." Lessons in English
are also given for the Japanese students. Ten hours
of classes a week are required, and the tuition is
Yen 10.00 per month, or Yen 28.00 per term.
Keisen Jogakuen
Miss Michi Kawai, a leader among Japanese women,
conduots a school for young women. called Keisen Jo-
gakuen, at l090 Funabashi, Chitose-mura, Kitatama-gun,
Tokyo-fu, which is in the outskirts of Tokyo. Most of
Miss Kawai's pupils are Japanese girls but she has taken
an interest in second-generation girls as well and has
about ten of them as students. She has lived in the
United States, having obtained a part of her education
there, and understands the problems which confront the
second-generation girls in Japen. It is said that
Yen 60.00 a month covers all a student's expenses at this
school, except clothing and incidentals.
Other Schools for Second-generation Girls
Miss S. Kawasaki, representing the Young Women's
Buddhist

=== Page 35 === (confidence 0.99)
-26 -
Buddhist Association, at Musashino Joshigakuin, Kiohi-
joji, Tokyo, takes an interest in second-generation
girls, and the Tokyo Women's Christian College hes
about ten second-generation girls.
It may be said in general that girls are not en-
couraged to pursue higher learning in Japan as they are
in the United States. School for girls try chiefly to
give them information which will prepare them for mar-
riage. A smell amount of higher learning is considered
sufficient for the average Japanese woman. Cultural
subjects, such as flower arrangement, tea ceremony,
Japanese art, history, et cetera, are included in the
curricula of such schools, but it would not be pos-
sible for a student to go very far in the study of the
sciences or of the art, culture, and history of other
nations.
There are also several privately operated com-
mercial schools for girls, but no special facilities are
offered for second-generation students.

ORGANIZATIONS
INTERESTED IN SECOND-GENERATION JAPANESE
International Cultural Relations Association.
(Kokusai Bunka Shinkokai)
Meiji Seimei Building, Hibiya
This organization is headed by Count Kabayama and
operated as a Foundetion, in a menner similar to that
of the Rockefeller Foundation. It is interested in
second-generation Japanese only to the extent of spread-
ing the knowledge of Japanese culture. One of its prin-
cipal activities is establishing libraries of volumes
regarding Japan and Japanese culture in all countries
of the world; probably its greatest efforts in this
direction are being made in the United States at the
present time. It also provides a few scholarships for
foreign students to come to Japan for study.
The organization is building up its library in
its headquarters in Tokyo, and has about 3,Oo0 volumes.
It is also engaged in trenslating Japanese ciassics into
English. It may assist second-generation students in
Jepan by providing funds for securing proper teachers;
this is being considered at present.
While many prominent Government officials are in-
terested in this organization, it is not officially a
Government organization.
For further comments as to the aims of the Kokusai
Bunka Shinkokai see Enclosure No. 1.(a).
Institute

=== Page 37 === (confidence 0.99)
- 28 -
Institute for Education of Over-sea Japanese
(Kaigai Kyoiku Kyokai)
This organization aims to provide a school, dor-
mitories, health clinic, educational guidance, and a
olub for foreign-born Japanese. The school conducted
under the auspices of the Institute is discussed in
the seotion on "Sohools" of this report.
The Imperial Department of Education is said to
have given Yen l00,000 to the organization, and large
sums are said to have been collected from other sources.
While many Government officials are interested in
the organization it is not an official Government pro-
ject. The project is still in a formative stage and
its future is uncertain.
America-Japan Society
(Nichibei Kyokai)
Imperial Hotel, Tokyo
This organization is formed by leading Japanese
statesmen and civilians and leading American residents
of Japan for the purpose of Japanese-American friend-
ship. It has its headquarters in Tokyo and there are
affiliated organizations in other parts of Japan. The
Society takes an interest in anything which tends to
further its aims, but generally its activities are con-
fined to holding banquets in honor of outstanding Japan-
ese and American officials or civilians. Its functions
are generally very formal.
In

=== Page 38 === (confidence 0.99)
- 29 -
In February l935 the Society gave a party on
Linooln's birthday to the second-generation students
in Tokyo. One of the floors of a Mitsubishi depart-
ment store was used, decorated to simulate an American
county fair. About 400 of the young people attended.
The funotion served to bring to the attention of both
the Japanese and American members of the Society the
large number of second-generation young people study-
ing in Tokyo and caused much comment. Nothing further
has been done by the Society for the second-generation
students.
Tokyo Y. M. C. A.
The Y. M. C. A. provides a school, es described in
the preceding section of this report, for second-gener-
ation students. It also offers all the usual facil-
ities of the Y. M. C. A., such as limited darmitory
space, recreation rooms, gymnasium, employment service,
and personal guidance. It cannot devote itself solely
to these Americans, however, as it is primarily interest
ed in the Japanese.
Tokyo Y. W. C. A.
The Y. W. C. A. provides the usual Y. W. C. A. facil-
.ities (gymnasium, recreation rooms, employment service,
and personal guidance) for the second-generation girls
who call upon it. One employee, a second-generation girl,
is

=== Page 39 === (confidence 0.98)
-30 -
is assigned solely to that work. Its main funotion,
of course, is connected with Japanese girls.
Foreign Service of the United States of America
The Foreign Service extends the protection and
assistance of the Government to the second-generation
Japanese in the same manner as to any other American
citizens, and no efforts have been made to give spe-
cial attention to the second-generation Japanese.
One or two of them are employed in the Embassy and
consulate general at Tokyo and two are employed in
the consulate at Yokohama. The second-generation
Japanese should be a cause for further study, how-
ever, as their numbers in Japan increase and their
possible influence upon Japanese-American relations
grows important.

SECOND-GENERATION JAPANESE
WHO FORSWEAR JAPANESE NATIONALITY
An appreciable number of second-generation Japan-
ese feel sufficiently satisfied with American citizen-
ship to renounce formally their claim to Japanese na-
tionality. The following information, from page 49 of
the 1935 JAPAN-MANCHOUKUO YEAR BOOK is of interest:
"Expatriation of Japanese
"Until l9l6 Japan did not recognize
the expatriation of her sons or daughters
who acquired foreign citizenship, except-
ing those females who married foreign sub-
jects. The result was the Japanese who
legally became American citizens, for ex-
ample, still figured on Japanese census
register so that they stood on the pecu-
liar status of double nationality. This
procedure was at last changed and the Law
of Nationelity was revised in August l9l6.
The law was further amended in December
1924 and the foreign countries to which
the expatriation applies was designated to
be, (1) U. S. A., (2) Argentina, (3) Brazil,
(4) Canada, (5) Chili and (6) Peru. It
may be noted that those American or Canadian-
born Japanese boys not yet expatriated are
still technically liable to the Japanese
conscription law, so that the crux of "double
nationality" question remains unsolved,as
is also the case with the Prussian or French
boys born in America.
"The number of those Japanese subjects
who on account of their having acquired citi-
zenship in foreign countries lost their Japan-
ese nationality under the revised Nation-
ality Law in recent years is as follows (the
bulk being those who have become American
at lith? oer
citizens):--

STATISTICS
Immigration and Emigration
There are no satisfactory figures available show-
ing the numbers of native-born Americans of Japanese
parents who are now living in Japan. The Japanese im-
migration officials at Yokohama, and presumably at
other ports, classify all persons of Japanese race who
arrive here as Japanese subjects regardless of place
of birth or citizenship and the fact that they may bear
American passports. There are no satisfactory Japanese
records of emigrants and none whatever of persons who
leave the country bearing American passports.
The book by Professor Edward K. Strong, Jr., en-
titled The Second-Generation Japanese Problem (Stanford
University Press, l934), was used as background for
this report. In this volume the author states that
despite the most painstaking investigations it is im-
possible to obtain accurate statistics of immigration
and emigration of Japanese aliens or Japanese-American
citizens, for either the Hawaiian Islands or the con-
tinental United States. The Department of State is no
doubt familiar with the unreliability of the Department
of Labor figures prior to l928, at least, as regards
Mexicans. The same inaccuracies exist, according to
Professor Strong, in statistics concerning Japanese.
The following figures are taken from Professor
Strong's book (pages 73 and 76),with his sources indicated:

* * *
According to the above statistics, from l92o to
1930 Hawaii gained 6,764 American citizens of Japanese
race from the Orient and 976 from the United States,
while it lost 14,3ll to the Orient and 1949 to the
United States; which resulted in a net loss of second-
generation Japanese from Hawaii of 7,547 to Japan and
973 to the United States.
On the mainland, during the same period, 3,646
Japanese-Americans are reported as having arrived from
Hawaii and none from Japan, while 2,369 are reported
as having gone to Hawaii and 3,834 to Japan. These
figures are obviously not complete since no arrivals
are reported from Japan. They are also presumably in-
accurate as they do not agree with the figures from
Hawaii.
There are no figures for Japanese-Amer icans either
arriving in or departing from Hawaii or the United States
prior to 1920, except 1,612 proceeding from the mainland
to Japan during the period 1910-1920.

The outgoing second-generation Japanese from
Seattle are not tabulated by the immigration authori-
ties in that port. I have estimated the Seattle
figures by assuming that the inooming and outgoing
figures would be in the same proportion at that port
as at San Francisco and Los Angeles.
For the mainland therefore, during the years
1930-l934, inclusive (disregarding the difference
between fiscal and calendar years), there is a sur-
plus of 1436 going to Japan over those coming to the
United States. From Hawaii the total surplus of out-
going is 2074 for the years 1931-1935, inclusive.
From the mainland and Hawaii together the total sur-
plus for the years 1931-1934 is 3162, or an average
of 790 per year. The preponderance of outgoing occur-
red in 1933-1935, while there was an opposite tendency
1n 1930-1931,
How long such a surplus of second-generation
Japanese has been accumulating in Japan it is not pos-
sible to say; it seems, however, to indicate a small
but steady movement, and to represent a permanent loss
of population.
It will be seen that, of the outgoing for the en-
tire period, 75 percent were under 16 yeers of age, while
of those returning to American territory the ages over
and under 16 years were almost equelly divided.
Census

=== Page 48 === (confidence 0.99)
-39 -
Census

Japanese race living in Hawaii and the United States,
the following figures from page l60, Chapter VI, of
Professor Strong's book are of interest:

According to investigations made by Professor
Strong, the second-generation Japenese in both Hawaii
and United States average ten years of age. Of those
in the United States some 7,497 would be of voting age
in 1935; there would be 11,289 of voting age in 1937;
and 50,000 in 1951. There are no similar statistics
for Hawaii.
Out of 5,127 second-generation Japanese inter-
viewed in California, l05l had visited Japan for an
average of five years and only four had spent any time
in Hawaii. There are no similar statistics of second-
generation Japanese in Hawaii. The above figures in-
dicate that a high percentage of second-generation Jape-
ese have visited Japan. If applied proportionally to
all of them living in the United States and Hawaii, it
would indicate that some 13,670 now living in United
States

=== Page 49 === (confidence 0.99)
-40-
States and 18,230 now in Hawai1, or a total of 31,900,
had visited Japan and returned to the United States by
1930. There is no way of telling how many of these
people were in Japan at one time, but it is fair to
assume that 10,000 is not an exaggerated figure.
Birth Rate of Second-Generation Japanese
and Possible Number Who Return to Japan
Professor Strong points out in his work that,
contrary to the popular belief, the average birth rate
among Japanese immigrants living in the United States
is only about three children to a family. Moreover, a
comparison of the birth statistics with the l930 census
of American-born children of Japanese parentage indi-
cates that almost one third of these children either
return to Japan or die before reaching the age of thirty.
This, of course, tends to hamper any alarming increase
in the number of persons of Japane se race residing in
American territory, at least for the present.
During the years 1890-1930, inclusive, some 106,562
persons of Japanese race are estimeted to have been born
in the United States. From 1900 to 1930, 109,899 persons
of Japanese race are estimated to have been born in Hawaii.
This makes a total of 2l6,46l births. The estimated
deaths of all persons of Japanese race (whether born in
American territory or abroad) during the 1900-1930 period
are 34,819 in Hawaii and 31,864 in the United States, mak-
ing a total of 66,683 deaths. The difference between the
total of 216,461 births and the census which showed 159,542
second-generation

=== Page 50 === (confidence 0.99)
一41 -
second-generation Japanese then living in United States
and Hawaii, is 56,919. This figure represents not only
those who may have been in Japan or abroad at the time
but also those who died.
Of the total deaths in the entire United States in
1927, 28.3 percent were of persons 24 years of age or
less (Statistical Abstract, 1931, page 78). Assuming
that the same percentage of persons of Japanese race
in the United States died before reaching the age of
25, and that the number of such deaths might roughly
represent the number of American-born persons of Japan-
ese race who died between 1900 and 1930, it would ap-
pear that, of the 66,683 persons of Japanese race who
died in American territory during that period, 18,871
(28.3 percent) were second-generation Japanese, that
is, American-born. Deducting this figure from 56,919
(the difference between the number of births and the
1930 census figures for American-born persons of Japan-
ese race), the result is 38,404, which might be taken
to approximate the number of second-generation Japanese
probably living in Japan in 1930. Even this figure,
which is doubtless far from accurate, is impressive.
It will be realized, of course, that with the few
and unreliable statistics available it is impossible to
arrive at an accurate estimate of the number of second-
generation Japanese who are living in Japan. Although
some students of the subject have estimated the number
as high as l00,000, it would appear from the deduction
made

=== Page 51 === (confidence 0.99)
-42-
made in the preceding paragraph and the statistics
for the past six years furnished by the United States
Immigration Service (page 37) that the figure is
probably nearer 40,000.
Whatever the figure may be at present, however,
the second-generation Japanese with whom this report
is espeoially concerned (those who have obtained an
American education and are here temporarily, designated
above as "Group C," page 5) do not exceed 1,000 in
number,according to estimates made by persons who have
interested themselves in the young people. Upon their
return to the United States, the influence of Group
C second-generation Japanese is more political than
economic, their number is relatively small and they
ocoupy a higher position scially than those who re-
turn to work as agricultural laborers. Mr. Dickover's
reports dealt with the other second-generation Japanese
living in Japan.

=== Page 53 === (confidence 0.99)
- 44 -
Enolosure No. 1.(a)
The Japane se Government
The Japanese Government appears thus far only
mildly interested in the second-generation Japanese,
but the Foreign Office has shown some interest and
has studied the situation created by the students
now in Japan with the purpose of giving them as favor-
able a view of Japan as possible. The Government
recognizes the opportunity of making such American
oitizens the agents for spreading favorable infor-
mation of Japan upon their return to Hawaii and the
United States. It is a natural tendency and its
development should be interesting to observe. Thus
far, however, it is said that most of the young people
return to their American homes thoroughly glad to get
back to an environment to which they have become ac-
customed.
The Government's interest is expressed in finan-
cial assistance to the Institute for Education of Over-
sea Japanese (Kaigai Kyoiku Kyokai), for example. It
is probable that similar assistance is given other
agencies or schools which are considered to be doing
work which meets with the approval of the Government.
Japan is now endeavoring to spread its culture
over the world in much the same way as Christian nations
in the past tried to convert the pagans and at the same
time spread their cultures and extend their political and
economic

=== Page 54 === (confidence 0.98)
- 45 -
●conomic influence. Japan's aims are as ambitious as
those of any nation in Europe or America, in either
anoient or modern times. The Pax Romana, the German
Kultur, and the Soviet dreams of world revolution find
a perfect counterpart in modern Japanese thought. Ideas
of world domination are not new in Japan, although they
may be new to most occidentals. The method of approach,
under the guise of spreading culture through establish-
ing libraries,granting scholarships, and establishing
professorships, is not obnoxious, as many of the methods
of the Christian nations were in the past. Much of the
culture of Japan can be made use of to advantage by the
rest of the world. As long as Japanese expansion is
confined to purely cultural attempts there can be very
little objection. The important thing is to recognize
such activities for what they are, however, and to keep
their effect under control.
* * *

=== Page 55 === (confidence 0.98)
- 46 -
Enclosure No. 1.(b)
Assistance from Americans in Japan
The American colonies in Japan have thus far taken
scarcely any interest in the second-generation Japanese.
A few Americans have done so as individuals, and the
American members of the America-Japan Society took part
in the Linooln Birthday-party. One missionary takes a
leading part in the Japan Cultural Forum. An American
teacher in Waseda University and one of the Americans
in the Tokyo Y. M. C. A. are also active with the second
generation.
Otherwise neither the Americen missions, mission
schools nor American clubs take an interest. There would
even be considerable opposition on the part of some of
the old American residents, i.e., business men, to admit
second-generation Japanese as members of the American
clubs of Tokyo, Yokohama, and Kobe.
* * *

=== Page 56 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. 3.(0)
COPIED FROM
JAPAN TIMES & MAIL FRIDAY, JUNE 21, 1935
FAMOUS DRAMA BY KIKUCHI KAN GIVEN IN ENGLISH
American-Born Japanese Present Play of Samurai Era
At Seinenkai-kan
The Pacifio Young People's Society of Tokyo gave a
most ambitious porformance when they presented Kikuchi
Kan's three-act play, "Katakiuchi Ijyo," at the Nippon
Seinenkai-kan, Aoyama, on Thursday evening. The English
translation of Glen Shaw's "Better Than Revenge," was
used, and it was a novel experience to hear American as
it is spoken combined with a story of revenge of the
samurai days with scenes and costumes from the Kabuki
stage.
The sharp contrast of language and a Japanese play
was felt as something so out of the conmon that the
audience frequently laughed when the American inter-
pretation did not elways unite as smoothly es intended
with the Japanese plot. But on the whole the effort was
successful.
Sugimura es Ichikuro
Mr. Satoru Sugimura carried off the honors of the
play as Ichikuro, a retainer of & feudal lord, who ac-
cidentally kills his master and escapes with Oyumi, a
concubine of the mester. The sword contest between Mr.
Sugimura and Mr. Toshio Tsukahira showed that American
born Japanese have inherited a respect for the steel
blade which they crossed in a samurai manner.
Miss

=== Page 57 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
Miss Himeko Nishikawa was seen as Oyumi, but her
flute-like tones did not suggest the hardened and sordid
woman the playwright piotured. She gave her lines well,
every word distinot and clear, which could not be said
of the most of the players. But she did not succeed in
portraying the thoroughly bad female who drives Ichikuro
along the downward path.
Mr. Sugimura and Miss Nishikawa were seen to ad-
vantage in the rest house scene in the Kiso mountains.
Miss Nishikawa rose to the occasion as the nagging part-
ner of the ronin encouraging him to kill two wayfarers for
their money and clothing. His disgust and revolt after
the deed was well expressed by the leading actor and his
final decision to leave the woman and give up his evil
life was done in a dramatic manner.
Familiar Tunnel Scene
The tunnel soene at Yabakei in Kyushu, reminiscent
of the performances of the Kabuki actors, was simple but
well staged.
Mr. Sugimura after having acted as a samurai, then
a highwayman, was seen as a venerable priest who has spent
20 years of his life making a tunnel through the rocks as
an atonement for his past, and a blessing to the people of
the countryside. His scene with Mr. Kazuo Ikeda as the
son of the feudal lord who has spent years in tracing the
slayer of his father was impressive, and the modern senti-
ments regarding revenge were most interesting.
The last scene was dramatic as the priest with his
remaining strength broke through the last wall of rock
and

=== Page 58 === (confidence 0.98)
-3 -
and the moonlight pours in, while the samurai forgives
and forgets in a noble manner.
Large Cast of Players
The cast was made up of peasants, stone cutters,
travellers, taken by the following:
Miss Kimie Yamada, Mr. Hajime Watanabe, Mr. Sho-
iohiro Matsumoto, Mr. Tadao Yamada, Miss Hatsumi Ohama,
Mr. Makio Teshirogi, Mr. Yutaka Nii, Mr. Michikata Nishi-
moto, Mr. Toshio Tsukahira,Mr. Taketo Abe, and Mr. Masaru
Akutagawa.
Most of the actors mouthed their words or turned
their heads away from the audience so that their lines
could not be heard well. They showed a lack of familiar-
ity with the mannerisms and gestures of the Kabuki actors,
but they more than made up for these defects common to
amateurs by their earnestness.
The play was produced by Mr. Shiro Okakura, and
Professor Paul Rusch was chairman of the production.
The stage manager was Mr.Tatsu Tanabe, and his assistant,
Mr. Tadao Kaneko. Miss Kiyoko Kuroishi and Miss Lily
Mizuguchi acted as prompters.
This group of young people born on the Pacific coast
of America and Hawaii in giving this play by Kikuchi Kan
have shown a willingness to attack one of their pressing
problems, to raise funds that the members may satisfy their
desires to cooperate in the study of cultural subjects,
visit the famous places of the country and accomplish
special research work. Their first effort has been crown-
ed with success, and should lead to a larger development in
the future.

THE SCHOOL OF JAPANESE
LANGUAGE AND CULTURE
THE JAPANESE LANGUAGE SCHOOL was opened in
class rooms of the Foreign Language School, Hitotsubashi,
Tokyo, on October 1, 1913, with professor Frank Muller as
Director. The Schcol was given formal recognition by the
Department of Education on October 7th of that year.
Article III of the Constitution reads:
"The object of the School shall be to teach the
Japanese language to foreigners and to conduct
courses of lectures on Japanese History, Literature,
Religion, Institutions, Customs. etc."
In June, 1930, the name was changed to the Scoor oF
JAPANESE LANGUAGE AND CULTURE.
LOCATION OF THE SCHOOL
The School is located in the City Y.M.C.A. Building,
Mitoshiro-cho, Kanda, Tokio, which is most conveniently
reached from the central part of the City. The School
occupies the third floor for class rooms and a part of the
fourth floor for offices. All inquiries should be made to the
Executive Secretary's Office Room, No. 419 on the fourth
floor.
LANGUAGE DEPARTMENT
Method of Teaching
The School is committed to the direct method of lang-
uage teaching. The fundamental principle is that in the
beginning the students shall be caused to hear many times
simple but natural Japanese sentences, so far as possible
of such a nature that the meaning can be visually demon-
strated.
Students in the first term are expected to become able
freely to read and write both of the Japanese syllabaries
(hira-kana and kata-kana). During the three year course
—4-

=== Page 73 === (confidence 0.99)
they are expected to master the Chinese characters ap-
pearing in the first eleven national readers (tokuhon).
However, the Director and Faculty may excuse students,
on application, from the writing and reading of Chinese
characters.
Hours of Instruction
The regular hours of instruction are from 9 a.m. to 6
p.m., except Saturdays and Sundays. Evening classes will
also be arranged if there are sufficient number of students.
All regular students who take at least two periods of
instruction per day are classed as regular students. Special
students may take as many hours as they wish. Credits
will also be counted towards Diplomas.
Certificates and Diplomas
I. Those who wish to obtain the School's certifcate to
show the degree of attainment in language study
may do so upon passing examin tions prescribed by
the Faculty of the School. For such examinations a
fee of Yen 10.00 will be charged.
II.
Those who wish to enter an advanced class in the
School are tested by special examinetions provided by
the Faculty of the School. An examination fee of
Yen 10.00 will be charged.
III. Requirements for Diplomas: In order to secure the
School's Diploma for any given year's work, the fol-
lowing requirements must be met:
1. For students taking the equivalent of three hours
a day for five days a week under School supervision,
three terms, for those taking two hours a day, four
terms.
2. In hours, a total of at least 400 hours for the year.
3. In terms of credits (one credit representing one
hour's work a week for one term); a total of 45 cre-
dits. In this way, students taking any number of
hours of regular work can secure credits toward a
—5—

=== Page 74 === (confidence 0.99)
Diploma for any given year, but thc year's work must
be finished within two years.
4. In Japanese Cultural Studies, a report of at least
5,000 words in some field of study approved by the
School.

week, for an average of twelve weeks per term:
a. In class of three or more

¥120.00

COURSES OF STUDY
General Explanation
For the first two terms of the First Year all regular
students follow the same courses of study. From the be-
ginning of the third term students begin to specialise in
vocabulary and sentence forms according to their needs
and interests. Since in the earlier years most of the stu-
dents were interested in religious work, detailed courses
have been worked out for those students. For the non-
missionary students, teaching materials suited to the needs
of such individual students will be chosen in consultation
with the individual students. Substitutions for regular
courses may also be made according to the needs of indivi-
dual students, but in each case the proposed substitute
material must receive the approval of the Dean in order to
be credited towards a Diploma.

EXTENSION DEPARTMENT
All students, especially first year students, are strongly
urged to take work at the School in Tokio. For advanced
students and beginning students who find it absolutely
impossible to come to Tokio, the School's courses are offered
through the Extension Department.
The students in the Extension Department have the
same privileges as in the main School in Tokio and the
School is determined to do its best to give them as nearly
as possible the same advantages as resident students.
Courses of Study and Diplomas
The courses of study for the students in the Extension
Department are exactly the same as those of resident stu-
dents at the main School, which means that all students
have the benefit of working towards Diplomas.
As has been explained, the School uses the direct
method, because Japanese is being taught as a living lang-
uage, and therefore it is necessary that a very large part
of the work be done with a teacher, using the ear just as
much as possible. The number of hours per week indicated
in the schedule means, of course the actual number of
hours spent with the teacher and does not include hours
spent by the student studying alone. Of course it is recog-
nized that those who are not in or near Tokio will have
added difficulties, and for that reason the School is willing
to allow more time for the completion of each year's work,
with the following conditions: (1) Total number of hours
ultimately given to any section of the work will be equal to
that given by the resident students; (2) The year's work
must be finished within two years. (See also page 5).
Reports
The School furnishes report blanks (Shindo Shühō) on
which the teacher should send in a weekly report of what
has been done that week. These are simple and quickly

=== Page 78 === (confidence 0.99)
filled in, but they are very essential if the School is to be
able to do its part.
There are no examinations in this Department, but in-
stead the student is expected to send in once a month
something he has written in Japanese without the help of
his teacher and without the teacher's corrections. This is
to help the School to know the student's progress.
Teachers
It goes without saying that the local, personal teacher
is a most important factor. Wherever possible we should
advise that teachers be used who have at some time had
training in one of Mr. Matsumiya's special teacher training
classes.
Tuition Fees
The fees for the Extension Department are Yen 40 a
year if paid in advance, or Yen 14 a term for each of the
three terms of the first and second years, and Yen 21 a
term for each of the two terms in the third year. For this
fee the School furnishes and sends all material when
needed, and keeps and examines reports from the student
and his or her teacher, writes letters both to student and
teacher when necessary and gives diploma or certificate
when the work is finished.
Credits in Foreign Institutions
The School certificate for language work has frequently
been accepted for credit by American colleges. The School
is also investigating the possibility of securing credit for
studies carried on under the direction of the Cultural De-
partment.
—10—

=== Page 79 === (confidence 0.99)
CULTURAL DEPARTMENT
On June 24, 1932, the Trustees approved a plan for the
further development of this Department, providing (a) for
organizing and maintaining regular lecture courses and
study classes in Japanese history, religion, history of Japa-
nese art, general culture and present day problems; and
(b) for introducing foreign students to libraries and other
institutions and to Japanese specialists.
A Bureau of Information and Serrice is maintained
with the following functions:
a. To provide information concerning existing institu-
tions working in the various fields of Japanese history,
culture and current problems, this work to include the
preparation of a brief descriptive list of libraries,
museums and other institutions open to foreign stu-
dents.
b. To make definite arrangements for research students
to secure the benefits of existing institutions, and to
introduce them to specialists and to other students
working in related fields. Tokyo libraries are open to
students introduced by the School of Japanese Lang-
uage and Culture.
To render personal service to foreign students in se-
curing interpreters, translators, living accommoda-
tions and in making travel and other business
arrangements.
d. To provide information as to what has already been
done in any special field of research.
e. To secure educational advisers for resident and special
research students, and where desired, to cooperate
with advisers chosen in preparing reports on work
accomplished, to be sent to home institutions with
which research students may be connected.

THE SCHOOL FOR
FOREIGN-BORN JAPANESE
Qualification for Admission
Foreign-born Japanese men and women who wish to
study the Japanese language and culture will be accepted
as students. However as a minimum educational require-
ment, all applicants are expected to be graduates of high
schools.
Classes
The completion of the regular two year course is re-
quired for graduation. The two year course is designed to
give students the ability to read, write and speak the
Japanese language. There is also an advanced class for
those who wish to do research work in Japanese culture.
Courses and Hours of Instruction
A. REGULAR COURSES.
The courses are divided into two groups, required and
elective. All regular students take three periods of class
work every day, consisting of two required hours of Japan-
ese language and one hour elected from the cultural group.
Students must take not less than fourteen hours a week.
The classes for language study are very small, with not
more than five students in a class, so that individual atten-
tion can be given to every student.
B. SPECIAL COURSES
Japanese Language: Regular courses in reading, writ-
ing, composition and conversation will be provided for
special language students from 1 to 3 p.m.
Cultural Courses: Those who wish to undertake cul-
tural studies may join any of the cultural courses with the
regular students.
——13——

=== Page 82 === (confidence 0.99)
COURSES OF INSTRUCTION
Japanese Language
1. READING—In order to acquire basic ability in read-
ing, the National Readers (Tokuhon) will be taught reg-
ularly. It is also planned to read as widely as possible, for
example, newspapers, plays, novels, poetry, etc. This course
will be given four hours a week.
2. WRITING—Chinese characters in ordinary use will
be taught in connection with reading lessons. It is expected
that students will acquire not less than 1500 different
characters within one year. This course will be given three
hours a week.
3. COMPOSITION—Students will be asked to write orig-
inal compositions assigned by the teachers, both in the
class-room and at home (Sokudai and Shukudai). Teach-
ers will correct them and criticize the construction of the
sentences and explain the words.
4. CONVERSATION—One of the chief difficulties of
foreign-born Japanese is that they cannot use the right
word for specific occasions. Therefore in this course, differ-
ent types of expression in daily conversation will be ex-
plained. In addition to this study, students will also be
drilled in the use of the Tenioha (particles). This course
will be given one hour a week.
5. TRANSLATION—In order to develop ability to ex-
press correctly and freely English sentences in Japanese
this course is given one hour every week.
6. JAPANESE GRAMMAR—This course will be given for
advanced students one hour every week.
Courses in Japanese Culture
1. History of Japanese Civilization (given throughout the
year). Lecturer: Mr. Ozeki.
2. Japanese Geography (given in first term).
Lecturer: Mr. Mizuno.
3. Japanese Classical Plays (not given this year).
Lecturer: Miss Ishikawa.

and third terms). Lecturer: Mrs. Doi.

year). Lecturer: Mr. K. Matsumiya.

Lecurer:

11. Shuji (given throughout the year).

12. Visits and Observation Trips.
In connection with the cultural courses, students visit
various places in Tokio and vicinity, such as museums,
libraries, historical places, factories, theatres, etc. These
trips are made on alternate Fridays, after 2 p.m. Students
can also attend the English lectures given in the Language
Department of the School.
Personal Guidance for Foreign-Born Japanese
The School will make any necessary investigation for
parents who wish to send their children to Japan. The
Executive Secretary will be glad to meet any foreign born
Japanese who wish to begin Japanese language or cultural
studies. He will also endeavor to make any necessary ar-
rangements regarding living accommodations or school
entrance for foreign born Japanese. The Information
Bureau of the School will gladly assist those who wish to
undertake special lines of research.
The School is hoping that a well equipped dormitory
may be provided if sufficient funds are secured. One of the
most serious questions for those who stay in Japan after
graduation is the employment problem. The School will,
as far as possible, assist students to secure positions on
—15——

school, is always glad to meet any students to talk about
their personal problems.

WASEDA INTERNATIONAL INSTITUTE
There are a large number of American-born Japanese,
and Americans of Japanese lineage, who graduate from
high schools but give up their college careers because of the
depression and other economic prcblems that make it dim-
cult to secure positions after graduation. Recent industrial
developments in Japan offer new oppcrtunities that are
drawing many of them to Japan, and their peculiar quali-
fications in language and culture, for positions requiring an
understanding of both America and Japan opens up for
them many places in companies engaged in trade between
the two countries.
However, both for the requirements of business, and for
the needs of those who wish to come to live in Japan, boys
and girls in this group find themselves lacking in knowledge
of both Japanese language and customs, and in need of
special preparation not available through the ordinary
school. Two years ago Waseda University, the largest uni-
versity in Japan, opened its doors to Japanese from abroad,
and foreigners of Japanese lineage, but, in order to take
advantage of this opportunity, many need special language
preparation.
To meet this need Waseda Hoshien, at the request of the
President of Waseda University, has organized its Inter-
national Institute. In this Institute courses will be given,
beginning in September 1935, which will prepare high school
graduates from abroad for entrance into Waseda Univer-
sity.
Students will be admitted at the beginning of the spring
and fall terms, and, though the Institute seeks to serve the
needs of those who intend to enter Waseda, students pre-
paring for other universities will be accepted. (See Regula-
tions of this Institute).
This education of Japanese from abroad and of foreign-
ers of Japanese lineage is in the pioneer stage. For this
reason this Institute is anxious to make the opening years
-1-

=== Page 87 === (confidence 0.99)
successful ones, and only students who wish to go on with
their higher education, and whose school and character
records are satisfactory, will be accepted.
Waseda does not admit students without examinations,
though all students must be graduates of high schools (see
Catalogue of Waseda University for entrance requirements),
and, on the average, at least two school terms will be neces-
sary for the language preparations for those coming from
abroad. In many cases a year or more will be necessary,
but for the student who comes with considerable knowledge
of Japanese it should be possible to take the entrance ex-
aminations of the University the following March after
entering this Institute in September.
It is not advisable for a student to decide to come to To-
kyo unless he is prepared to give his best to the work here
and to persevere until he is successful. The language diff-
culty demands a sacrifice of time and effort that is only
worth while if the student is going to put it to use.
This Institute office will be glad to answer inquiries and
supply information to those who are considering coming to
Japan for study, and to assist them in establishing them-
selves in the Waseda community.
Americans of Japanese lineage, with a mastery of both
Japanese and English, and with the virtues of both Japanese
and Americans, have important tools which are needed to-
day. Not only in America and Japan, but in the inter-
national field, they will be able to take a place of promi-
nence and leadership. Indeed they have an opportunity to
make stronger the bonds between the two countries and to
establish relationships on a footing that will assure per-
manent peace. Waseda International Institute hopes that
many will pass through its doors into this important work.
-2-

=== Page 88 === (confidence 0.99)
REGULATIONS
of the
WASEDA INTERNATIONAL INSTITUTE
CHAPTER 1. GENERAL BULES
Article 1. The purpose of this Institute is to prepare for
entrance into Waseda University (Department of Political
Science and Economics, Law, Literature, Commerce, and
Engineering, Department of Specialization (Senmonbu),
Higher Normal Department, and First and Second Junior
Colleges (Kötögakuin, etc.), and to instruct in Japanese
Culture foreigners of Japanese lineage and other persons
who wish to enter Waseda University from foreign schools.
CHAPTER 2. CURRICULUM OF INSTRUCTION
Article 2. The school year is divided into three terms:
an Autumn Session, Winter Session, and Spring Session.
Article 3. Courses of instruction are given as follows:
Japanese Language (Reading, Grammar, Composition,
Dictation, Writing, Conversation).
Chinese Language (Reading).
English (English-Japanese, Japanese-English).
Mathematics (Algebra, Geometry).
Courses in Japanese Culture.
Article 4. Four hours of instruction are given in the
forenoon, and one hour in the afternoon.
CHAPTER 3. SCHOOL TERMS AND HOLIDAYS
Article 5. The Autumn Session is from the beginning of
September to the end of December, the Winter Session from
the beginning of January to the middle of March, and the
Spring Session from the beginning of April to the middle of
July.
-3-

=== Page 89 === (confidence 0.99)
Article 6. The Summer Vacation is from the middle of
July to the beginning of September, and the Winter Vaca-
tion from the end of December to the beginning of January.
Article 7. School will be closed on Saturdays, Sundays,
Japanese national holidays, and school holidays.
CHAPTER 4. ADMISSION AND DISMISSION
Article 8. Regular students will be admitted at the
beginning of the Autumn and Spring Sessions, but students
may be admitted at other times by special permission of the
President.
Article 9. Qualified students will be admitted on the
following basis:
1. Students who are graduates of high schools or who
have the equivalent academic standing.
2. Students who have been recognized by the Minister
of Education in Japan according to Public Notice No. 266 of
the Department of Education, issued in 1929, as students
whose scholastic attainment is equal to or above that of
middle school(chugakko) graduates.
Article 10. A candidate for entrance must submit an
application for admission, a recent card-size photograph,
and the following:
1. A record of past studies.
2. A diploma or certificate meeting the qualification of
Article 9, 1. The certificate must give years and
courses of studies, and a record of the last three
years' school work.
3. A certificate of recognition from the Minister of Edu-
cation in Japan meeting the qualification of Article 9,
2. But this certificate is unnecessary for foreigners
of Japanese lineage.
4. An abstract of the census register.
Article 11. The student, on being permitted to enter
this institute, must submit a certificate with the signature
of a guarantor.
—4-

=== Page 90 === (confidence 0.99)
Article 12. A guarantor must be an adult in good stand-
ing who lives in the city of Tokyo or its vicinity.
Article 13. The student who wants to leave this Insti-
tute from sickness or some other circumstances must submit
a resignation with the signature of his guarantor.
Article 14. This Institute may expell any student for
the following reasons:
1. Violation of the Regulations or disgraceful or dis-
honorable conduct.
2. Bad character and conduct, without showing satis-
factory improvement.
3. Irregular attedance or absence for over one month
without giving justifiable reason.
4. Failure to pay tuition fees.
CHAPTER 5. TUITION FEES
Article 15. The student is required to pay a matricula-
tion fee (5 yen) at the time of entrance.
Article 16. Tuition fees (includes the expenses of text
books, teaching materials, and physical training facilities)
are to be paid at the beginning of each term as follows:
Autumn Session
60 yen
Winter Session
45 yen
